#166. The engine parts that 'fight the laws of the universe'

A jet engine will have hundreds of turbine blades

Every time you fly, an extraordinary feat of endurance occurs inside the aircraft's engines.

Hidden from view inside the sleek outer nacelle are hundreds of little turbine blades that can shrug off enormous mechanical stresses and temperatures exceeding 1,500C.

It is a true engineering marvel. And these hardy blades are not sculpted or cut from their material â they are grown.

During the 1960s, engineers worked out how to take a molten metal alloy, cast it into the shape of a turbine blade, and then cool it very carefully from one end to the other over a matter of hours. A single crystal or grain of metal alloy forms during this process of gradual, delicate solidification.

Like turning a cup of water into one big snowflake, of your own design, rather than a mini blizzard of many smaller snowflakes.

"It's about as complicated as manufacturing gets," says Ben Fifoot, manufacturing engineer at Rolls-Royce, which makes turbine blades in the UK. "It's a real motivator to get out of bed in the morning."

Only a handful of companies around the world can produce these single crystal turbine blades, and the process has been perfected end-to-end in just five countries, including the US and UK. Engine-makers continually vie for access to the technology.

Rolls-Royce, GE and France's Safran are among the few firms that have perfected the process.

In September, GE Aerospace announced, external it would acquire its turbine blade supplier for $11.75bn (Â£8.78bn). Neither company responded to BBC News' requests for comment but the move has been described as an effort to ensure supply of these critical components.

Separately, in March, Rolls-Royce said it would invest more than Â£19m in its Rotherham blade casting facility.

Turbine blade specialists continue to work on improving the capabilities of this little-known technology. All the while, China is desperate to prove that its engineers can produce turbine blades of the same quality as those in the West. Who will come out on top?

"Demand for [turbine blades] is huge. So few companies have managed to do it, which does give it this kind of magical aura," says Bill Sweetman, an aviation industry writer and consultant.

He suspects GE Aerospace's recent move is about securing reliability and predictability in the notoriously volatile aircraft manufacturing supply chain.

The casts play a crucial part in the cooling process

But why is the single crystal casting process, which has been around for decades now, still so specialist?

To grow one of these blades, asserts Fifoot, is to "fight the laws of the universe".

This is because each molten blade would naturally cool into a solid object consisting of many small grains of metal alloy, all jumbled together. But you don't want a crumbly sugar cube, you want a perfect diamond.

Getting a single crystal to grow into the shape of the blade instead isn't just a matter of cooling the molten version down slowly, the process also uses a bizarre trick called a pigtail.

The pigtail is a small spiral at one end of the blade. Fifoot says that, at the beginning of the cooling process, this spiral "chokes" the flow of solidifying crystals so that just one takes over. A bit like a hairpin corner in an F1 race right before the finish line â one driver can take the lead here and go on to win.

"Sometimes we need to play around with the number of turns or the angles on [the pigtail] to try and get more favourable grain," adds Fifoot. Once the blade is formed, this crucial but sacrificial spiral is cut off.

France's Safran is among the few companies that can make single crystal blades

Blades made by Rolls-Royce are used in aircraft engines including the Trent XWB-84, which powers the Airbus A350-900, and the Trent 1000 XE, for the Boeing 787.

The single crystal is essential to ensure each blade's performance. Sometimes a blade doesn't cool quite as planned, for example two crystals form within it instead of just one, and the metal alloy must be reprocessed so that the manufacturer may re-use the material.

Special ceramic coatings also protect blades from extreme temperatures inside engines. And another important feature is that blades are cast with tiny channels that allow a flow of cooler air to coat them in a kind of film while the aircraft engine is working.

"The drive at the moment [across the industry] is currently to make those cooling passages really intricate," says Paul Withey, at the University of Birmingham, who formerly worked for Rolls-Royce. The more turbulent those little flows of cool air are, he explains, the more they will knock heat out of the surrounding, much hotter air.

And blade-makers also seek to reduce the mass of these components as much as possible.

"If we can take any weight out of it, you can run that engine more efficiently using less fuel â you can spin it faster and hotter," says Fifoot.

The turbine blades are used in the Trent 1000 engine among others

Meanwhile, China hopes to become only the sixth country to master the whole technology chain for single crystal blade casting.

A report in May, external quoted one engineer at the AECC Beijing Institute of Aeronautical Materials claiming that China now had a single crystal alloy "superior to or equivalent to the second-generation single-crystal superalloys widely used in Europe and the US". It costs less to make, too, apparently.

But the West has a "huge advantage" over China given that the majority of commercial airliner production happens there, says Sweetman. Supply chains and the latest innovations are generally clustered in the West. Plus, Western manufacturers also have lengthy safety records under their belts.

China must compete on practically every aspect of aircraft manufacturing, external â by no means just on turbine blades â in order to make serious in-roads. "You can definitely see that happening but it's a long, uphill struggle," adds Sweetman.

Demand for aircraft is currently high, which is already straining various supply chains in the industry. That demand could grow higher still, suggests Withey.

During the next few years, one of the two main aircraft manufacturers, Airbus and Boeing, could launch a new short-haul plane, external, he says. Then, the other would have to follow suit in order to remain competitive.

"There'll be a bun fight," says Withey, over who gets to supply the engines and, by extension, those all-important single crystal turbine blades.

#165. Poppi founders are going after PepsiCo's Gatorade with new beverage startup Jetty

After disrupting the soda category with Poppi, founders Allison and Stephen Ellsworth are now taking aim at hydration with their new brand, Jetty.

Starting Monday, Jetty drinks and ready-to-mix electrolyte packets will be on shelves at Whole Foods grocery stores and available online at Target and Amazon.

The new, self-funded venture directly pits the Ellsworths against PepsiCo, which bought Poppi for $1.95 billion in 2025. Pepsi owns Gatorade, which dominates the U.S. sports drink category with about 60% market share, according to Numerator data.

Poppi shook up the soda category with prebiotic fiber, low calorie and sugar counts and its fun marketing aimed at Generation Z and millennials. Jetty looks to upend the hydration category through a similar strategy. To do so, it will face off not only against Gatorade but also a swarm of other upstarts looking to get in on the electrolyte-fueled trend.

For decades, Gatorade and other sports drinks have pitched their products to athletes who need electrolytes and fluids after an intense workout, although many consumers drink them every day without even sweating. Jetty is aimed at people who need a hydration boost, but not the same level of electrolytes or sugar as a traditional sports drink.

"No one has really created a product for just everyday hydration — literally everyday hydration for normal people," Allison Ellsworth, Jetty's chief brand officer, told CNBC. "Like I woke up this morning, I didn't run a marathon."

Following Poppi's sale to PepsiCo, Ellsworth stayed on as an advisor to the brand, although she said she is no longer involved in the day-to-day operations.

After 10 years building Poppi, Ellsworth said, she finally had free time. As Ellsworth tells it, she went to the doctor for the first time in a decade. She started using a GLP-1 medication and lost 60 pounds.

Ellsworth's husband, Stephen, now CEO of the new brand, noticed she wasn't hydrating, even when she came back from the gym.

GLP-1 medications such as Ozempic and Mounjaro can make people less thirsty. But because staying hydrated can also help mitigate some of the drugs' side effects, such as nausea and headaches, it can become more important.

"I physically struggle getting liquid into my body," Ellsworth said.

Plain old water is often less appealing to consumers, even dehydrated GLP-1 users, but more flavorful options with added benefits can be tempting. About 44% of GLP-1 users report consuming more functional beverages, according to EY-Parthenon's third annual survey of more than 3,600 current or former GLP-1 users.

Ellsworth sees a broader appeal for Jetty, too.

"Our core audience is going to be millennial moms and Gen Z," she said. "Gen Z just isn't relating to these old school brands, the legacy of the Gatorades and the Powerades. They're just not cool."

Consumers broadly have become more interested in staying hydrated.

Dollar sales of hydration and electrolyte products climbed 20% to $2.6 billion over the 52 weeks ended Oct. 4, based on data from SPINS, which tracks new universal product codes, or UPCs. The category also has a strong repeat buyer rate of 62%.

However, the Ellsworths aren't the only ones to notice the potential in hydration. Big food and beverage companies have been looking to hydration, too.

Kraft Heinz debuted Capri Sun juice pouches and Kool-Aid packets with electrolytes this spring. In April, Pepsi unveiled an overhaul of the Gatorade brand, focused on making it more appealing to a wider swath of consumers, with more potential occasions like a "fresh morning start" or an "afternoon walk." And Electrolit is a top performer for its distributor Keurig Dr Pepper, with 40% retail sales growth in the company's third quarter.

All of that means more competition for shelf space and consumers' "share of throat," an industry term for beverage market share.

To increase Jetty's visibility across the grocery store and to maximize convenience for shoppers, Jetty will be available in both powders and bottles.

Single-use electrolyte mixes are growing at a much faster clip than the ready-to-drink category, Ellsworth said, thanks to Unilever's Liquid I.V. and smaller upstarts like LMNT. On Amazon alone, powders are a $1 billion category, according to Ellsworth.

The ready-to-drink, or RTD, sports beverages are a much larger market, but the category's sales are shrinking as consumers reach for other hydration options instead. Unit sales of sports drinks have fallen 9.1% from 2022 to 2026, according to Numerator data. And Coca-Cola has written down the value of BodyArmor by more than $1.7 billion after buying control of the brand for $5.6 billion in late 2021.

"Me and my husband know how to do RTD," Ellsworth said.

Jetty will launch with five flavors, including watermelon, orange mango, fruit punch, black raspberry and white cherry. All of them contain 100 milligrams of sodium, 310 milligrams of potassium and 100 milligrams of magnesium in a 15.2-ounce bottle or 15-gram packet.

In addition to electrolytes, Jetty products also contain B vitamins, 3 grams of fiber and 6 grams of sugar. It doesn't use artificial sweeteners or dyes, its founders say.

For comparison, a 20-ounce bottle of traditional Gatorade Thirst Quencher has about 270 milligrams of sodium and 80 milligrams of potassium, plus 34 grams of sugar. The sports drink brand is also phasing out artificial colors and promoting lower-sugar versions.

"What's really interesting about this product and this opportunity is we now can capture all those lapsed drinkers — or that generation that didn't even ever drink it in the first place," Ellsworth said.

Poppi's new owner knows about Jetty and the market it is trying to capture, too.

"I don't think [Pepsi] gets it, but what they do get is that the movement is changing, and they think that just by taking dyes out or doing 90% less sugar, they're going to capture this audience," Ellsworth said. "But that is very big corporate thinking."

She added that she believes Pepsi has missed the mark on advertising to Gen Z consumers across its portfolio, such as in its campaigns for Sprite competitor Starry.

Perhaps as an extension of that, Poppi's performance has been underwhelming under PepsiCo's ownership.

The food and beverage giant now estimates the fair value of potential performance-based payments to Poppi's prior owners is $12 million, Pepsi said in a regulatory filing in October. At the time of the deal, Pepsi promised an additional payment of $300 million if Poppi hit certain targets by the third quarter of 2027. The Ellsworths declined to comment on the filing to CNBC.

This time around, with one successful brand under their belts, Jetty's founders say they have learned from past mistakes. Those lessons should help Jetty as it launches at a much larger scale than Poppi did.

Three different claims lawyers have signed off on Jetty's packaging, according to Ellsworth. Poppi paid $8.9 million to settle class-action litigation alleging false advertising over its "gut healthy" claims.

For now, Jetty is entirely self-funded with $2.5 million, although Ellsworth said that the brand will likely raise money in 2027. This time, though, she said investors will probably look less like traditional venture capitalists.

"I want to bring in investors that bring value," Ellsworth said. Instead of a venture capitalist who is willing to invest $15 million, Jetty would rather consider investors who can only write a check for $1 million but have access to celebrities. "So then you're getting access to talent on a cheaper level and people that are invested in the brand."

And Jetty has also learned a few lessons from Gatorade. For example, the brand is already at work on a lemon-lime version — the original flavor of Gatorade.

"We'll eventually do it," Ellsworth said. "But it was really important at the beginning just to stick to flavors that we love, for it to taste good."

#164. Government's EV targets expected to be reduced this week

The government launched a review into its policy to ban new petrol and diesel car sales by 2035

The UK government's electric vehicle (EV) targets are expected to be reduced this week in a move likely to be welcomed by carmakers but opposed by environmental groups.

Under the current rules, 80% of new cars sold in the UK must be electric by 2030 but industry groups have long called for "urgent relief".

New petrol or diesel cars are due to be phased out by 2030 and by 2035 all new cars must be zero emission.

But the speed of the transition to electric has been hotly debated, including within the cabinet.

The government has been consulting on reducing its 80% rule and a significant reduction is now expected.

Downing Street has insisted no decision has been made.

A government source told the BBC it would approach the issue in a "pragmatic and balanced" way.

If ministers do decide on a steeper watering-down of the rules, it will arguably be interpreted by some - alongside the approval of the Jackdaw gas field in the North Sea - as the government prioritising economic considerations over environmental ones.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham has recused himself from this issue due to his wife's links to an electric vehicle company.

The key meetings on the subject will instead be chaired by Cabinet Office minister Louise Haigh.

Asked about reports the targets would be reduced, Burnham dismissed this as "speculation", adding: "No decision has been made at this point."

Sustainability groups say any weakening of the 2030 target will threaten the UK's long-term electrification and climate goals.

It could undermine the UK's claim to be providing leadership on climate just as ministers head to Turkey for COP31, the UN's climate summit.

Energy UK, the trade association for the energy industry, has also opposed the move.

Last month, it said, external reducing targets could damage "the investment certainty needed to deliver the infrastructure, innovation and services required for the transition to zero-emission transport".

Asked if climate change was no longer a big priority, Health Minister Karin Smyth said the government was committed to its emissions targets.

"This country is doing great work in terms of our transition and looking at renewables â that's a key part of growth in different parts of the country," she told BBC Breakfast.

She added that the prime minister would have "a good story to tell" when meeting other leaders at COP31.

A government spokesperson said: "We are committed to the phase out of all new cars relying solely on petrol and diesel engines by 2030, and to 100% of new car and van sales being zero emission by 2035, and our legally binding net zero targets."

The Society of Motor Manufacturers and Traders welcomed the government's review of the EV targets when it was announced in the summer.

Last week, head of the association Mike Hawes said transitioning to electric vehicles had come at a "heavy cost" with billions spent on discounts to drive demand in electric vehicles.

He said the government's targets could not be achieved "without putting at risk any future investment".

UK electric car sales target set to be weakened

Electric vehicle sales targets could be cut after pressure from car makers

EU waters down plans to end new petrol and diesel car sales by 2035

Net zero means balancing the amount of planet-warming "greenhouse" gases produced by human activities with the amount being actively removed from the atmosphere.

The government's policy to phase out petrol and diesel vehicle sales has undergone multiple changes since it was first introduced by former Prime Minister Boris Johnson in 2020.

His successor Rishi Sunak pushed back the timeline to 2035, and the Burnham government set up a review of the EV sales targets in August.

Sunak introduced phased targets for EV sales in the UK, known as the Zero Emission Vehicles (ZEV) mandate.

Under the ZEV mandate, the percentage of new car sales that need to be EVs increases each year. The target was 28% for 2025, 33% for 2026, and so on until it reaches 80% by 2030.

Pressure in the European Union saw the bloc scrap a similar plan to ban new petrol and diesel car sales by 2035, particularly from countries with large auto industries such as Germany.

The Conservative Party has also vowed to abandon the petrol car ban if it wins the next election, with leader Kemi Badenoch calling it a "well-meaning, but ultimately destructive piece of legislation".

Green Party leader Zack Polanski said the government was "building up a shocking record of backsliding and bad decisions on climate issues".

#163. Trump says Iran war to end 'fairly soon,' plays down prospects of new strikes

U.S. President Donald Trump said on Monday that the war with Iran could end "fairly soon," adding that Tehran was "talking very politely" with Washington.

Trump told reporters in the White House Monday, after a meeting with Qatari Prime Minister Sheikh Mohammed bin Abdulrahman Al Thani, that "We're seeing momentum toward the end of this conflict. I think it's going to be ending fairly soon."

"Iran militarily is effectively over," Trump said, citing the destruction of its navy and surging inflation that he said has hollowed out its economy.

Qatar, Saudi Arabia, UAE, Bahrain, Kuwait would "prefer the status quo" which involves maintaining the naval blockade against Iran and "Operation Iron Gate" — a task force announced on Saturday aimed at preventing Iran from using ground routes to transport advanced weapons and other materials — Trump said in a Truth Social post.

He reiterated that Iran would not be allowed to have a nuclear weapon and kept the door open for potentially resuming strikes should circumstances change. "America is 'docked and loaded' and ready to go," he said, though caveating that new strikes were "quite unlikely."

The conflict, now in its ninth month, has driven a surge in fuel prices, hitting nearly every sector of the global economy.

The U.S. had reportedly planned for a potential increase in naval forces in the Middle East in the run-up to the midterm elections, with Trump hinting at escalating the conflict after the results.

The administration has since toned down the war rhetoric after bruising midterm election results that saw Republicans lose both chambers of Congress as voters expressed frustration with soaring living costs stemming from the war.

A diplomat briefed on the Qatar meeting told Axios that Trump told Al Thani that he wanted a deal and would give talks a chance, but could reverse course if Iran does not take the negotiations seriously.

Iran has put forward a two-day proposal to the U.S. via Qatari mediators, Reuters reported, citing a senior Iranian official, demanding lifting of the sanctions imposed on Tehran and a new shipping route in the Strait of Hormuz. Washington was reviewing the proposal, according to the report.

Iranian officials have disputed that talks with Washington were underway. Mohsen Rezaei, secretary of Iran's Supreme National Security Council, reportedly said that Washington should stop "manufacturing fake news about imaginary talks."

Oil prices edged lower on Tuesday on the prospect of de-escalation, extending losses from the previous day. U.S. West Texas Intermediate dropped over 1% to $85.8 per barrel, after sliding more than 4% in the prior session. Brent futures dropped more than 1% to $91.56 a barrel, hovering near 10-week low levels.

#161. Trump’s C-SPAN call marks latest surprise post-midterm appearance

President Donald Trump made an unexpected call into C-SPAN's evening broadcast Monday, extending a series of surprise appearances after Republicans' midterm election defeat.

"It looks like we've got a call from the White House," host Mimi Geerges told viewers as Trump joined the debut of "Washington Journal Primetime."

Trump congratulated Geerges on the launch before taking questions on immigration, Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth and the Iran war.

Asked what he watches on C-SPAN, Trump said he follows viewer call-ins to gauge public sentiment.

"I like to watch the call-ins because it gives me a little of an indication of where we are," he said.

The unannounced, eight-minute call followed another unexpected appearance earlier Monday, when Trump entered the White House briefing room alongside UFC CEO Dana White. Reporters were summoned so quickly that aides did not have time to attach the presidential seal to the podium, according to Reuters.

It was Trump's first briefing room appearance in 198 days, according to a White House pool report. With the press secretary post vacant, Trump joked about offering White the job, to which the UFC chief declined.

Trump said he had been having "a very bad press day," as he floated White as his next press secretary.

Last Wednesday, a day after Republicans suffered substantial midterm losses, Trump also abruptly invited reporters to the construction site of his White House ballroom for his first public event since the election.

Trump spent more than 20 minutes discussing White House construction and renovation projects, including the ballroom's Corinthian columns, marble flooring and the helipad, before turning to the election results during questions from reporters.

When asked whether he regretted how his campaign had ended, Trump distanced himself from the Republican losses.

"If I were on the ballot, we would have won," he said.

Democrats won control of both the House and Senate in the Nov. 3 elections, dealing Trump a major political setback and setting up a divided government when the new Congress takes office in January.

Trump has long favored direct telephone contact with the media, including live calls to Fox News programs such as "Fox & Friends" during his first term.

CBS News previously reported that Trump personally arranged one such television appearance, while The Associated Press has documented his habit of contacting journalists directly by cellphone to pitch stories and react to live coverage.

— CNBC's Daniel Mangan contributed to this story.

#160. Doing these things now will help you save money at Christmas

How soon is too soon when it comes to thinking about Christmas and your finances?

The answer, of course, is that it is never too soon. Save early and the cost becomes more manageable.

The squeeze is harder for some than others, with charity Barnardo's warning that some parents will skip meals so their kids get a Christmas dinner with the trimmings.

But, debt advice charity StepChange is offering tips to everyone, to avoid Christmas spending leading to a financially painful new year.

The best way to avoid a difficult January, it says, is to make a realistic plan before the spending begins.

Small decisions now can make a big difference later. Its tips include:

Budget for Christmas in full. So, that's not just presents, a budget should include the cost of food and drink, travel, parties, presents, postage, decorations and other festive extras

Spread the cost. Don't just rely on your December paypacket. Also, make a shopping list before Black Friday on 27 November - it is only a discount if you were going to buy it anyway

Book your train journey now. Advance tickets, if available, are cheaper

Set spending limits. Put a cap on the bill for Christmas socialising, and have a quiet conversation with family and friends about how much to spend on presents

Stocktake before the big food shop. Check your cupboards, fridge and freezer before you go shopping and plan meals around food you already have. Make a list of what you need and compare prices.

Think twice. Can you afford what you are going to buy? Can you afford the repayments if it's bought on credit? And, do you really need it?

"Christmas is a time when we want to enjoy ourselves and make things special for the people we care most about," said Vikki Brownridge, chief executive of StepChange.

"But it can also put a lot of pressure on household budgets, and it is key to take steps to get ahead of any January financial blues."

If you are concerned about Christmas leaving you in debt, then it is best to seek advice well before things become unmanageable.

The independent MoneyHelper website has a guide to finding free debt advice near you, external.

There are also tools to work out if you are receiving all the benefits and grants you are entitled to, and tips on how to draw up a budget.

And it is really important to watch out for scams at this time of year. Fraudsters will take any opportunity to catch you out, especially when you are under pressure financially.

#159. Restaurant chains refocus on food quality to bring back diners

Juicier chicken tenders. Revamped sauces. Richer and creamier Frosties.

Across the restaurant industry, chains are turning their focus back to their food in the hopes that better quality will win back diners.

Over the last two years, consumers have dined out less frequently and grown pickier about where they choose to eat, based on data from Black Box Intelligence. In that time, "value" has been the buzzword du jour for restaurant executives, who have explained to analysts and investors that the word encompasses more than menu pricing alone.

Now, industry CEOs are turning to their corporate chefs to try to beat rivals on quality and taste, as they look to expand their definition of value. Discounts and one-off promotions can lift traffic for a time, but the actual product still matters for restaurants' long-term growth, as well as franchisee profitability.

From Wendy's to Chipotle Mexican Grill, chief executives touted new or upcoming improvements to menu items on their quarterly earnings conference calls in recent weeks.

"We think the product's improved and even better," Chipotle CEO Scott Boatwright told analysts on the company's call in late October, referring to the burrito chain's brisket, which returned to menus for a limited time Thursday.

For some chains, better food is a key step in ambitious turnaround plans.

For example, "quality food at real value" is one pillar of Wendy's five-part turnaround strategy that the chain unveiled on Thursday.

"We're also rebuilding our food offerings and have begun testing improvements to quality and craveability of our core items, including thicker, crispier bacon, a richer and creamier Frosty, whole leaf lettuce on sandwiches and improved salads," Wendy's CEO Bob Wright told analysts on the call Thursday.

That same day, the company reported its seventh straight quarter of same-store sales declines and publicly rejected a $1.4 billion takeover bid led by Nelson Peltz's Trian Partners. Wendy's said the proposal did not take into account the potential value that its turnaround plan could unlock.

There is precedent to suggest that better-tasting food can contribute to higher sales.

In 2009, Domino's Pizza changed its recipe, swapping out its sauce, adding garlic to its crust and switching to shredded mozzarella with a hint of provolone. Along with blunt commercials that compared its previous crust to cardboard and old sauce to ketchup, the changes helped to spur U.S. same-store sales growth of 9.9% the following year.

Now, some of the same team that led the Domino's turnaround is copying the playbook at Burger King's U.S. restaurants.

Part of the burger chain's multiyear comeback plan has focused on revamping its core menu items. It started earlier this year with its flagship offering, the Whopper, which received a better bun and a new mayonnaise. And in September, it rolled out an overhaul of its nuggets, switching to 100% white meat, crispier breading and a more flavorful marinade. It also reformulated its sauces.

"We've tried to look at our biggest categories first," Josh Kobza, CEO of Burger King parent Restaurant Brands International, told CNBC. "We've also thought about the ones where the fixes are more straightforward and less operationally involved."

Following the changes, Whopper sales were up by a double-digit percentage in the third quarter, Kobza said on the company's earnings call in late October. And nugget sales climbed more than 35%. Burger King said its U.S. same-store sales jumped 8.4% in the quarter.

Like Domino's, Burger King is highlighting diners' past gripes with its Whoppers and nuggets in its ads, which star Burger King U.S. president and former Domino's executive Tom Curtis.

Better-tasting food isn't just for chains that are in trouble.

Even Chili's, which has seen its same-store sales soar 52% over the past three years, is improving more of its existing menu options. After tackling its baby back ribs, burgers and nachos, it will turn next to salads and steaks, Brinker International CEO Kevin Hochman told CNBC.

"We're going to be doing more dry aging of our beef to make it more tender, versus doing it in a plant," he said. "It's a little bit more expensive, but we think the taste is going to be a whole lot better."

Chili's rival Applebee's launched a juicier chicken tender during its latest quarter, according to John Peyton, CEO of the chain's parent company Dine Brands. In 2027, Applebee's plans to overhaul more of its protein options.

"We think proteins are, number one, our biggest opportunity," Peyton told CNBC. "Number two, they're a big focus of the way people are thinking about eating right now, leaning into proteins and a little bit less with the carbs."

Chicken is also a focus for McDonald's, which is testing hand-breaded wings, strips and a sandwich in a handful of Chicago-area restaurants. It plans to expand the trial to more locations in the U.S. and Ireland in early 2027.

Moreover, McDonald's is trying to improve its coffee, with fresher beans and upgraded equipment, executives said at the company's investor day in September. Both chicken and coffee are fast-growing categories, offering more opportunities to lift sales than McDonald's signature beef burgers.

While the burger giant isn't in full turnaround mode like rivals Wendy's and Burger King, its U.S. business has struggled in recent quarters after its value strategy didn't gain the traction the company had hoped. In the third quarter, its U.S. same-store sales fell 0.2%.

In addition to menu tests, McDonald's is also leaning into training to improve the quality and consistency of its core menu items. Starting in October, the company launched "Make It Golden," a multiyear training program for corporate employees, restaurant workers and franchisees.

Ultimately, improved menu items mean little if the recipe changes aren't well executed.

#156. What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

One of the Artificial Intelligence (AI) industry's most important figures has been accused of having an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, prompting an investigation.

US intelligence officials believed that Chinese businesswoman Alice Huang, who had a close relationship with Nvidia CEO and co-founder Jensen Huang, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report.

On Sunday, Huang admitted, external to having been "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children.

US tech giant Nvidia, one of the most valuable companies in the world, has opened an independent investigation into the reports. The firm said it does not believe US national security had been compromised - and added that Huang was committed to continuing to lead the company.

President Donald Trump said on Monday that the FBI is also investigating the affair.

The FBI declined to comment further when contacted by the BBC.

Trump said he spoke with Huang on Monday following reports that the US believed he had been targeted in a Chinese espionage campaign.

"I asked him that question, 'Did you have any idea she was a spy?' He said 'I had no idea she was a spy' and I really believe him," Trump told reporters.

Here is what we know about the affair and why it matters.

Trump says FBI investigating Nvidia boss's affair with alleged Chinese spy

Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

Jensen Huang (left) and Alice Huang - also known as Huang Le - pictured at an event

US officials believed that Alice Huang had been recruited to spy by China's top intelligence agency, the New York Times reported, external. Her current whereabouts are unknown.

Alice Huang, who is of no relation to Jensen Huang, was a top executive at Singapore-based data centre firm Megaspeed between March and November 2023. The firm had been suspected of helping divert Nvidia chips to China, a concern that Nvidia maintains was investigated and debunked.

However, Megaspeed is under investigation by the Singapore government regarding previous allegations that the company was circumventing US sanctions on the export of Nvidia's high-end chips. The BBC has contacted Megaspeed for comment.

The company told the New York Times last month its current leadership took the allegations seriously and had appointed external legal advisers to conduct an internal investigation.

Surveillance reportedly revealed communications between Alice Huang and an American citizen, who was subsequently identified as Jensen Huang through a process known as "unmasking".

It remains unclear what, if any, intelligence Alice Huang â who is also known as Huang Le â allegedly gathered and passed to Beijing. She did not respond to the paper's request for comment.

When the BBC asked Nvidia if the company was aware of intelligence concerns, the company said it was not.

"We trust that if we were the target of a genuine threat, the US government would have notified us," an Nvidia spokesperson told the BBC.

China has rejected the spying allegations.

Foreign Ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun told reporters on Monday at a regular daily press briefing: "We have already responded to this. The allegations about Chinese spies are entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China."

The New York Times reported in late October that Trump had been briefed last year on intelligence alleging Alice Huang was spying on China's behalf.

Asked by reporters on Sunday whether he wished he had "acted on the intelligence", Trump responded: "No, I don't think so. I think he's an honourable guy."

US Secretary of State Marco Rubio, meanwhile, refused to comment when asked about Huang. Last spring, the secretary of state reportedly oversaw the drafting of a memo that warned the Trump administration about Huang's relationship with the Chinese businesswoman, according to the New York Times.

Nvidia shares slide as US tech giant investigates CEO's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Why Trump is all-in on AI despite the warnings

What is AI, how does it work and why are some people concerned about it?

Nvidia is at the heart of US and China's AI race, which is why the billionaire's affair has sparked national security fears among American lawmakers.

Trump has said "whoever wins AI wins", and the fear is that if China gains the upper hand in AI, it will supercharge the country's military and surveillance capabilities.

The US advantage is in large part because of US companies like Nvidia, working with producers like the Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company (TSMC), which makes more than 90% of the world's most advanced chips.

Since 2022, the US has used various export controls to keep the top tech out of China, which it says helps retain America's competitive edge and protects its national security.

This means companies like Nvidia have been largely locked out of the world's most lucrative market, with Huang once saying that Nvidia's share of the Chinese market had gone from around 95% to effectively zero.

Huang has engaged in shuttle diplomacy between Washington and Beijing, hoping to find a US-approved way back in, and shaping US policy on China along the way.

Sunday's revelations may explain why Huang has failed to bring home the biggest prize of all to Nvidia: the Trump administration's blessing to sell all but its most advanced chips to China.

Huang has soared to international importance with his company

Born in Taiwan in 1963, Huang was nine years old when his family moved to the West Coast of the US. He graduated as an electrical engineer from Oregon State University, and then with a master's degree in the same field from Stanford University.

Huang then worked at US chip firm AMD before co-founding Nvidia. Known as "Daddy Huang" in Taiwan and once lauded as the "Taylor Swift of tech", he has a reputation as a workaholic and perfectionist with incredibly high standards for his staff.

He has an estimated fortune of $155bn (Â£115bn), according to the Bloomberg Billionaires Index.

"Jensen Huang is a tech visionary of the likes of Steve Jobs," Stephen Witt, the author of Thinking Machine, a biography on Huang, told the BBC, adding that he was responsible for creating AI hardware as we know it today.

Huang has a famously close relationship with Trump. At the recent AI summit at the White House, where 42 tech executives were present, the president was flanked by Elon Musk and Huang.

Trump said he had not spoken to the Nvidia boss since news of the investigation emerged.

"I just know he's a fine person, but you never know what's going on," Trump told reporters, adding that he had "done an amazing job for our country".

There is no clear successor for Huang at Nvidia

Founded by Huang and fellow electrical engineers Chris Malachowsky and Curtis Priem in 1993, the company was originally known for chips that process graphics, particularly for computer games.

Eventually it changed focus to AI, a field it now dominates.

Huang rescued Nvidia from bankruptcy on more than one occasion, and took risks on AI a long time before the rest of the world did, effectively building the infrastructure from scratch.

"Today when we think of that, we think of these giant data centres - none of that existed. There were no customers for the stuff he was building. In fact his first customer was Open AI at the time they had 30 people - he actually gave it to them, he donated it to them," Witt told the BBC.

Every notable tech company building AI tools and infrastructure, including Amazon, Meta, Google, and Microsoft, now use Nvidia chips.

Currently worth $4.5tn (Â£3.4tn), Nvidia is one of the most valuable companies ever.

For now, the firm's board appears to have little appetite for removing Huang, saying in a statement on Sunday that they have "confidence in Jensen and his leadership team".

Sources tell the BBC his removal is unlikely because there is so much money at stake.

There is no public succession plan for the 63-year-old. He has often said he hopes to continue in the role for decades, and Bloomberg reports he has created an informal leadership structure around him unlike a traditional company's hierarchy.

His children, Spencer and Madison, and son-in-law Nico Caprez work at Nvidia in senior roles.

Lori Huang accompanied her husband at the state dinner held by President Donald Trump for his Chinese counterpart Xi Jinping in September

Huang met his future wife Lori Mills at Oregon State University while studying electrical engineering in the early 1980s. The pair were lab partners.

They married in 1985, about eight years before he co-founded Nvidia, and have two children, Madison and Spencer.

In 2007, the couple founded the Jen-Hsun and Lori Huang Foundation. The charitable fund has seen its assets surge in recent years, driven by the soaring value of Nvidia shares, and is estimated to be worth more than $9bn.

Its donations include tens of millions of dollars to engineering programmes at Oregon State and Stanford University.

Witt told the BBC that Huang has gone to great lengths to present himself as a family man, adding: "That's very important to him".

"He praises his wife, he talks about her in very glowing ways," he added. "The fallout of this would be bad for anyone's family. But for someone who has gone to such great lengths to cultivate an image of being a family guy and who keeps his children very close, it is very bad."

Additional reporting by Tom McArthur in London, Ayeshea Perera and Tessa Wong in Singapore and Helen Davidson in Sydney

#155. Qantas to outsource 850 local jobs with most roles moving to India

Qantas CEO Vanessa Hudson apologised for the "hard news" of 850 job cuts

Qantas will outsource 850 local corporate jobs to consulting giant Accenture with most roles moving to India as the airline fast-tracks AI investment.

In an internal email to staff on Tuesday, the Australian company's CEO Vanessa Hudson said "this is hard news for some and I'm sorry for the uncertainty it brings".

The job cuts will begin mid-2027, she said, and start with finance, procurement, people and marketing roles shifting "mostly" to teams in India and "some in Australia" as the firm seeks to modernise its IT systems.

Hudson said the "scale and speed of change in technology and AI is something none of us has seen in our lifetime", and it was in talks with unions and staff on the plans.

The move comes days after the airline provided a market update, flagging fare increases and flight cuts amid higher fuel costs.

On its new multi-year deal with Accenture, Hudson said it was about "modernising" the airline's IT systems to become "more efficient".

Qantas spends more than AU$1bn (US$691m; Â£514m) a year on technology, she said, and the company's back-end systems needed to be upgraded.

"Like every airline, we run on technology built up over decades for a different era, including more than 1,000 legacy applications that were never designed to work together," she said.

The deal with Accenture means "we will redesign and simplify parts of how our corporate functions work and reduce duplication", Hudson said, so staff can "spend more time on work that creates value for our customers and the business".

The company said that "in time", the work that Accenture takes over will be "transformed further with automation and AI".

Staff affected by the job cuts will be redeployed "where possible", the firm added, including new roles in Australia.

Qantas - which employs about 28,000 staff in Australia and another 4,000 globally - said thousands of its staff already use AI in their daily work.

The BBC has contacted the Australian Services Union for comment.

#153. 3 unexpected reasons the October jobs report was so strong

A little-noticed category in Friday's blockbuster jobs report is challenging the consensus view about job losses caused by artificial intelligence.

At least for now, the data suggests the U.S. is gaining jobs as a result of the AI boom, not losing them.

And another category suggests the strong jobs numbers could have some staying power.

Nonfarm payrolls grew by 165,000 jobs in October, according to the Friday report from the Bureau of Labor Statistics. It was a surprising surge in hiring that blew way past Wall Street's expectations. Economists polled by Dow Jones had forecast growth of just 59,000 for the month.

Here are three surprising things you might have missed from the report.

Hiring for consultants surged in the month, sending the profession's total head count to a fresh record.

The labor force for management, scientific and technical consultants expanded by 17,300 jobs between September and October, the largest month-over-month increase in nearly two years. This category is a subset of the professional and business services sector tracked by BLS and accounted for 25% of the entire sector's gains for the month.

What's more, this workforce has grown by more than 45,000 jobs over the past year. That gain pushed the total employee base for these types of workers past 1.9 million for the first time on record, according to BLS data.

Hiring in the industry had largely stalled since 2023 following years of rapid expansion during the pandemic. Wall Street has questioned if the rise of artificial intelligence will curb demand for these services.

Those fears have led shares of consulting firm Gartner to lose more than half of their value over the past three years. Accenture and FTI Consulting have dropped about 18% and 9% over that period, even as the S&P 500 has climbed roughly 74%.

Now, some economists are speculating that the recent jump could be driven by businesses hiring consultants to help them adopt AI. Gartner forecast in September that worldwide AI spending would total $2.7 trillion in 2026, a nearly 50% increase year over year.

"The AI buildout and corporate interests to increase utilization will keep the technical consultants employed," said Jeffrey Roach, chief economist at LPL Financial.

Roach called the consulting sector a bright spot in October that is "worth watching." He noted that these jobs also saw double-digit growth in the late 1990s, as firms raced to implement new internet technology into their businesses.

So far, data suggests that AI may be adding more jobs to the labor market than it is taking away through automation. An assessment by Moody's Analytics found that AI has created a net 786,000 jobs since ChatGPT launched in November 2022, driven in part by data center construction.

"Wasn't AI supposed to destroy jobs? The most dystopian scenarios even called for mass unemployment," Mark Zandi, Moody's chief economist, wrote in a social media post on Monday. "That risk hasn't gone away, and AI-driven productivity gains could still shift into a higher gear. But for now, AI is adding to job growth, not subtracting from it."

Hiring of temporary workers also drove growth, which could signal that the labor market is heating up.

The subsector's payrolls grew by 18,600 in October, one of its largest month-over-month increases since 2022. This category is another part of the professional and business services sector and accounted for 27% of the sector's gains for the month.

The total size of this workforce is still well below record highs set in 2022. But headcount has grown roughly 3% since December and now sits at levels last seen in April 2025, the month that President Donald Trump's rollout of his plan for steep tariffs put corporate America on alert.

Economists see temporary hiring as a leading indicator in the labor market. Conventional wisdom suggests that companies that hire new workers on a short-term basis often transition them to full-time roles later.

That could be what's happening here, but the fact that the end of the year is approaching makes it harder to tell. The boost could be a result of hiring as the holiday shopping season approaches — and if employers are staffing up for the holidays, they are less likely to retain those workers when January rolls around. Such hiring hasn't typically happened this early, but the season creeps earlier each year. The BLS adjusts its numbers to account for seasonal trends, but won't immediately make an adjustment if the trend is changing.

One hint that holiday hiring may be shifting comes in a separate category of workers: couriers and messengers. This is another area that acts as a barometer for holiday labor demand, and just over one out of every 10 new jobs in the month was in this category.

That category "bears watching for reversal in the months ahead," said Jason Pride, chief of investment strategy and research at Glenmede, a wealth manager.

Hiring related to political campaigns in the final weeks of an expensive election cycle could have also boosted payrolls.

Employment in political organizations has historically spiked heading into midterm or presidential elections, according to a BLS analysis. Staffing levels tend to peak in October of those years, the data shows. Nonfarm payrolls expanded in the months before the 2018 and 2022 midterm elections.

But the vast size of campaign coffers this cycle may have translated into more jobs than before.

The 2026 congressional election is slated to go down in history as the most expensive midterm on record, according to an analysis from OpenSecrets, a money-in-politics watchdog group. Billions of dollars spent on campaigns may have translated into paid gigs that involve door knocking, phone banking or other election-related work.

#152. Poundland unable to buy new stock as fears over collapse grow

Poundland is unable to buy new stock to fill its shelves after being blocked by its owners from accessing cash in its bank accounts.

The discount chain is on the brink of collapse with one insider describing it as "running on fumes". It has until Thursday to secure a rescue deal.

About Â£50m has been frozen by its American owner Gordon Brothers, the BBC understands, meaning its 600 shops are running out of stock.

Pressure is building on Gordon Brothers, which bought Poundland for Â£1 last June, after the owner rejected a takeover bid that could have saved the thousands of jobs currently at risk. Gordon Brothers has been approached for comment.

Poundland has filed a notice of intention to appoint administrators, which protects it from its creditors until Thursday.

Access to funds was limited because of a tension between Gordon Brothers' dual role as both creditor and shareholder, a source said, but added they expected a relaxation of funds in the next two days.

Last week, Poundland boss Barry Williams told staff like-for-like sales, which exclude new store openings, were up 11.4% last week, which included Halloween, but said this was Â£1.5m ahead of budget.

He said the business made a profit of Â£18.1m in the year to September, which was a "strong recovery" from the previous year where the business made losses of Â£68m.

The latest annual accounts have yet to be audited.

Burnham 'ready to support' Poundland staff as retailer faces collapse

Poundland staff protest after chain approach administrators

However, Gordon Brothers has painted a different picture of the business, saying the chain remains "challenged and vulnerable to any potential downturn in future trading".

It said it had provided significant funding to support the company's ability to trade since it acquired the business.

It has also made Â£18m, from a combination of debt interest payments and other fees and charges.

On Wednesday, executives from Gordon Brothers will appear before the Business Select Committee with MPs probing whether they are genuinely pursuing a solvent rescue of the business, which has 11,000 employees, and whether every realistic step is being taken to protect jobs and stores.

The session is being held in private given commercially sensitive discussions are still under way with bidders.

On Thursday, the 10-day moratorium to protect Poundland from its creditors expires, but if there is a prospect of a solvent deal, the deadline could be extended.

If not, Poundland is set to collapse into administration and could be broken up.

A source familiar with the sales process said London-based Xyra Group was making "significant progress" in its last minute bid to buy Poundland and could now be the front runner.

An extension of Thursday's deadline now seems an increasingly likely scenario. But such a case would be difficult to put forward if Gordon Brothers continues to block funding.

One observer remarked "if Gordon Brothers is going in front of that committee, you don't want to be giving the message we're pulling up the drawbridge".

Even if a deal is agreed to sell the business as a whole, there is no guarantee a new owner would not reduce the number of stores, but suppliers to Poundland would prefer that outcome to hundreds of sites being sold off to other retailers.

There has been interest from the likes of Poundstretcher, Tesco and B&M in picking up stores which could mean continuing employment for many workers.

The central issue is whether a piecemeal sale would protect more jobs than a buyer willing to keep Poundland trading as a national chain.

#149. Eli Lilly’s Foundayo obesity pill eases sleep apnea in late-stage trial, paving way for approval

Eli Lilly on Monday said its obesity pill Foundayo significantly reduced the severity of obstructive sleep apnea, the most common sleep-related breathing disorder, in patients with obesity, in a late-stage trial.

The drugmaker plans to file by the end of the year for U.S. approval of the daily pill for moderate-to-severe obstructive sleep apnea, or OSA, in adults with obesity or who are overweight. The results make the pill — which rolled out in the U.S. in April — one of several GLP-1 treatments to show promise in treating the condition.

OSA refers to interrupted breathing during sleep due to narrowed or blocked airways, which can cause drops in oxygen and increases in blood pressure that are associated with serious complications, such as hypertension, heart failure and stroke. The condition affects nearly 1 billion adults globally, including 80 million people ages 20 and over in the U.S., according to the National Council on Aging. Most have never been diagnosed, Lilly said in a release.

Lilly's blockbuster weight loss injection Zepbound became the first medication to win U.S. approval for OSA in December 2024. Patients have limited treatment options outside of that drug and many use cumbersome machines that provide positive airway pressure, or PAP, to allow for normal breathing.

"Foundayo is already making a difference for people living with obesity, and with plans to submit these results to the FDA by the end of the year, we could have the opportunity to help people living with OSA," Dr. Thomas Seck, senior vice president of product development of Lilly Cardiometabolic Health, said in a release.

The trial tested Foundayo in two independent studies, the first of which included 363 patients who were unable or unwilling to use PAP therapy. The second study included 349 people who were on PAP therapy for at least three months at the time of screening and continued that treatment during the trial.

Patients in both studies who took Foundayo started at a low dose and increased it gradually at four-week intervals to different maximum dosages.

Researchers specifically examined how much the pill reduced the so-called apnea-hypopnea index, or AHI, which records the number of times per hour a person's breathing shows a restricted or completely blocked airway. The index is used to evaluate the severity of obstructive sleep apnea and the effectiveness of treatments for the condition.

People who took Foundayo and were not using PAP therapy had an average of 21.1 fewer AHI events per hour at 52 weeks, compared with an average reduction of 4.8 per hour for the placebo, according to the results.

Foundayo also met the trial's second goal in that group, leading to an overall AHI reduction of 43.8% compared with a decrease of 6.3% for the placebo. Lilly said 31.5% of the participants in the Foundayo group met the criteria for remission or improvement to mild OSA.

Meanwhile, patients who were on both Foundayo and PAP therapy had an average of 18.3 fewer AHI events per hour at 52 weeks, compared with an average reduction of 5.5 per hour for the placebo.

Foundayo led to an overall AHI reduction of around 27.5% in that group, compared with a decrease of 3% for the placebo. The company said 19.6% of the participants on Foundayo and PAP therapy also met the criteria for remission or mild OSA.

Foundayo also reduced what's known as the sleep apnea-specific hypoxic burden — a measure of how deeply and for how long oxygen levels fall during sleep. That metric fell by 55.9% in people not using PAP therapy and 45.8% in those using it.

Lilly said the pill also helped patients lose roughly 11% of their weight across groups at 52 weeks and helped to lower a marker of inflammation.

The company said the safety data and how well patients tolerated Foundayo were generally consistent with previous trials on the pill.

Around 12% of people on Foundayo who were not using PAP therapy discontinued due to side effects, compared to 3.8% with the placebo. Meanwhile, 6.8% of those on both Lilly's pill and PAP discontinued the drug due to side effects, compared with 0.6% in the placebo group.

The most common side effects were gastrointestinal, including nausea, diarrhea, constipation and vomiting, and were generally mild to moderate in severity. That's consistent with other GLP-1 medications.

#147. Civil servant who secretly held five jobs found guilty of fraud

Civil servant Kashim Chowdhury juggled multiple public sector jobs

A civil servant who secretly held multiple public sector jobs on full-time salaries has been found guilty of nine counts of fraud.

Kashim Chowdhury, from east London, had five government jobs that overlapped in the spring of 2023. He fraudulently earned more than Â£284,000 of taxpayers' money.

The prosecution accused the 56-year-old of ''deliberate, intentional dishonesty'' and of ''exploiting the system''.

After seven hours of deliberation, the jury unanimously found Chowdhury guilty of eight counts of fraud by false representation and one count of fraud by a failure to disclose.

The judge said Chowdhury faced a ''very significant custodial sentence''. He added he was refusing bail because of the significance of the crime and for Chowdhury's own wellbeing.

"This was sustained and deliberate dishonesty," said Sarah Melo, specialist prosecutor at the CPS Serious Economic Organised Crime and International Directorate.

"He knew he could not legitimately hold multiple full-time positions at the same time, but continued to conceal what he was doing."

A Defra spokesperson said Chowdury "abused the trust placed in him as a public servant", adding that it would use all available avenues to recover the proceeds of Chowdhury's offending.

The judge said Chowdhury faced a ''very significant custodial sentence''

Between 2020 and 2023 Chowdhury had eight full-time public sector roles that overlapped and earned him Â£350,000 in total.

The prosecution told the court that on application forms he provided inaccurate information about his employment status and failed to disclose to government departments that he was already a civil servant. Once he secured the roles he didn't tell his employers he was working elsewhere.

He was allowed to work entirely from home because he told his employers he was caring for his mother, whom he lived with in east London. She had dementia and was diagnosed with terminal cancer in 2023.

How a civil servant got away with juggling five jobs for so long

But rather than reduce his workload to look after her, he kept taking on new jobs. In the spring of 2023, when his mother was a few months away from death, he had jobs at the Department for Environment, Food and Rural Affairs, the Department of Health and Social Care, the UK Health Security Agency (UKHSA), the Department for Business and Trade, the Food Standards Agency and the Home Office.

During this period he was contracted to work 183 hours a week - more than the number of hours that exist in seven days.

Chowdhury said he had taken on so many roles because he feared losing his livelihood after experiencing insecure employment in the past. He was made redundant from a job at the Department for Education in 2013 and was given a diagnosis of PTSD.

He said he had not been thinking clearly when applying for jobs ''because of my stress, distress and trauma and due to my panic at the time".

But the prosecution barrister, Lucy Organ, told the jury: "He's lied to you in evidence, he's lied to you under oath, which demonstrates the level of deceit he has descended to."

A succession of his managers told the court about his poor performance. Especially damning was the evidence given by Dan McCartney who managed Chowdhury at Defra.

He said he had to "effectively tell him what to do the whole time". McCartney said that during video calls Chowdhury had his camera switched off blaming technical problems. McCartney said this went on for four to five months.

But McCartney said that because Chowdhury was not subject to any formal performance measures at Defra, he was given a permanent job and remained there for two years.

Chowdhury told the jury he had not worn make-up during a work video call

The prosecution argued that he used sick notes to cope with his impossible schedule. In March 2023, he was on sick leave from four of the organisations he worked for. But he kept working at the Home Office and did not tell them he was ill.

His line manager at the UK Health Security Agency (UKHSA), Aleesha Adams, organised a video call with him in May 2023. By this point he'd been off sick for three months and she wanted to make a plan to get him back to work.

She told the jury that at the start of the call he was coughing a lot and waving tissues up to the camera. She also told the jury that he had been wearing white make-up to try to look ill.

Adams told the court that as the meeting went on the make-up started coming off his forehead. ''He was sweating, it was like rubbing off so it was darker underneath and darker on the neck."

Chowdhury told the jury this was a smear and that he had not been wearing make-up.

After the call, Adams discovered that he had logged into the call using a Defra email address. When she challenged him about it he blamed a technical error.

Adams grew suspicious. ''I believed he was lying,'' she told the jury. She escalated her concerns up the chain at the UKHSA. But his fraudulent behaviour went on for several more months.

The government's National Fraud Initiative flagged suspected fraud to Defra in 2023. Following this, the Defra Investigations Team launched an investigation and as a result, Chowdhury was arrested and interviewed under caution in July 2023. He was dismissed by the civil service in September 2023.

Chowdhury has also been accused by his siblings of bullying their mother into increasing his share of the family home days before she died.

In a case at the High Court, Chowdhury's two brothers and sister allege that he put "undue influence" on Faijun Nessa to sign documents that meant he would inherit half of her home.

The four-storey, six-bedroom home in east London has been valued at Â£2.1m.

His siblings say their mother could not have understood what she was signing because she was "desperately unwell". In January 2023 she was given a terminal diagnosis of stage four metastatic colorectal cancer.

The claimants also allege that Mrs Nessa, who was nearly blind and could not speak English, was unaware of what she was signing because the documents were not translated into her native Bengali at the time they were signed.

Kashim Chowdhury denies the claims. That case is ongoing.

#146. Bilt bets on local businesses, hospitality and major sports franchises, expanding beyond residential rewards

A version of this article first appeared in the CNBC Property Play newsletter with Diana Olick. Property Play covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, from individuals to venture capitalists, private equity funds, family offices, institutional investors and large public companies. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

Bilt is taking its residential property technology out into the neighborhood, announcing the launch of two new platforms designed to help local businesses and event venues attract customers and build loyalty. It is essentially taking the operating system it built for apartment rents and mortgage payments and deploying it across local area businesses, including hospitality, restaurants and sports venues.

The first offering, Neighborhood OS, is an artificial intelligence-driven system that works with restaurants, retail, gyms and other local businesses to attract customers, offer rewards and encourage repeat business. It does this by connecting the business's existing systems, like reservations and payments.

"We're helping local businesses tap into more commerce by being able to reach new customers and accept those rewards as currency to spend," said Ankur Jain, founder and CEO of Bilt. "This is giving every one of those merchants their own Shopify-like platform for real businesses, so now they can run their own marketing campaigns. They can run their own loyalty campaigns."

One example of this might be that a restaurant near a customer's home would offer a perk, then if the customer takes it, the system would recognize that and track that customer to manage future interactions, like more rewards, reservations or guest services. The AI helps the businesses measure which perks worked and which didn't.

Bilt lists initial partners that include Altamarea Group, AM Parking, Lowe's, and Thumbtack. Bilt says it partners with 50,000 merchants so residents can use Bilt rewards at their stores. Businesses that already have rewards systems don't need to replace them. This gives small and midsized retailers an AI platform to use on their own.

They can run marketing offers on their own to drive demand from buildings on the Bilt platform. Examples might be offering a customer a free round of drinks on their 10th visit or pulling in local sports fans by running a watch party on game day. They could push that out through the platform. So merchants now have the same tools that Bilt had to offer its own rewards.

"Offline commerce is six times larger in spend, and it's run by mom and pops, but they have to work across 10 different systems. Everything is done manually for marketing, and there's no way to track what's working," said Jain. "You democratize the ability for now mom and pop to sell against some of the biggest companies online, because they have all those tools."

The second offering is a membership platform for organizations that already have a customer base. That could be sports teams, property companies, hotels and financial institutions. The organization keeps its brand and customer relationship. Bilt layers in additional technology to connect that organization's program with its participating merchants, giving them access to more benefits they don't have through existing programs.

Bilt lists initial members including the Houston Rockets, Phoenix Suns, Sacramento Kings, Related Companies, Hyatt, Constellation Brands and Bozzuto, among others.

"We believe the neighborhood is one of the greatest amenities we can offer our residents," said Toby Bozzuto, president and CEO of Bozzuto. "Through our partnership with Bilt, we have an opportunity to make that amenity an even more meaningful part of their everyday lives."

For professional sports, the idea is to connect fans beyond the stadium. Teams will be able to introduce branded fan experiences that combine loyalty, fan passes, concierge services and neighborhood commerce. This would include rewards at local restaurants, retail businesses and branded events outside the stadium or arena.

Bilt launched in 2021 as a rewards program for rent and mortgage payments. Tenants could earn both loyalty rewards points as well as build credit history. It later expanded into credit cards. It reports 8.2 million members and powers one out of every four apartment buildings, according to the company.

#144. Australia considers criminal penalties for rogue AI in wake of OpenAI hack

PM Anthony Albanese says the law needs to catch up with a "fast-changing world"

Australia plans to create a new AI safety regulator and has flagged criminal penalties for tech companies who do not comply, months after an OpenAI hack.

In September, Australia's Prime Minister Anthony Albanese revealed that a rogue OpenAI agent had "infiltrated" a government health website and other systems, and criticised the company for taking months to tell them about the hack.

On Tuesday, he released the 18 recommendations made after a "rapid review" into the breaches including new safety and security standards for AI companies such as mandatory reporting of hacks.

AI brings opportunities, he said, but Australia must prepare for the "new risks" from this "rapidly advancing technology".

"The law needs to get ahead of and, in some cases, make sure that it catches up with, what is a very fast changing world," Albanese said.

One of the recommendations suggests changes to criminal law to have a standalone "failure to prevent" offence .

This would criminalise instances where a company develops "an AI model that engages in harmful conduct" and "fails to take reasonable steps to prevent that harm".

The review also recommended "mandatory, time-bound AI safety reporting" of hacks.

This point addresses what Albanese had earlier called his "disappointment" in the months-long delay for OpenAI to tell his government about the breach.

In June, a statistics portal containing "non-sensitive" data from Australia's universal healthcare scheme Medicare was hacked by a rogue OpenAI agent.

OpenAI said it only learnt of the breach in August while reviewing "misaligned model activity" and emailed a general inbox of an Australian government agency on 10 September.

It took several more days before the email was escalated to Australia's cybersecurity centre, with a message then sent to a government minister ahead of the prime minister being told.

Albanese said he spoke to OpenAI boss Sam Altman and raised "Australia's extreme concern about this incident", with Altman acknowledging there were "issues with protocols" at OpenAI.

Days after the hacks were revealed, OpenAI delayed its rollout of a new AI model, citing safety concerns.

Why did an OpenAI system hack Australia's health system - and can it be stopped in the future?

OpenAI scraps rollout of new model over safety concerns

#141. Goodyear Tire CEO Mark Stewart steps down as company continues turnaround plan

Goodyear Tire & Rubber Co. announced Monday that CEO Mark Stewart would be stepping down from his role effective immediately.

Board member Roger Wood is taking over as interim CEO and president, Goodyear said.

The leadership change comes as the tire company is in the midst of a multiyear transformation plan.

Stewart was hired in January 2024 to lead the company, with the goal of revitalizing the legacy American business to make it more efficient and profitable.

The company has faced disappointing results thus far, reporting last week third-quarter results that saw net sales down 2.3% year over year and an adjusted net loss of $31 million. Goodyear failed to reach a 10% operating margin by the end of last year, which was a major goal of the turnaround plan.

Goodyear said it formed a special committee of the board earlier this year to conduct a "strategic review of the company's businesses and operations focused on identifying opportunities to strengthen performance and drive shareholder value."

It said Monday it will continue that work by evaluating strategic and operational initiatives that include further optimizing the company's business mix and footprint, improving efficiency and investing in new products and capabilities that support long-term growth.

"Goodyear has made meaningful progress in becoming a more efficient and focused company, and the board believes this is the right moment to transition leadership to accelerate that progress and capture additional opportunity," Goodyear Chair Joe Hinrichs said in a press release.

The company has faced headwinds from tariffs, higher raw material costs and increased competition from China.

Wood has been a member of the company's board of directors since July 2023 and was previously the co-CEO of Tenneco as well as CEO of Dana Holding Corporation.

The company will start a search for a permanent replacement as part of its strategic review.

Shares of Goodyear were down roughly 4% in early trading Monday.

#140. Polymarket says annualized revenue has topped $1.8 billion

Prediction market platform Polymarket has surpassed $1.8 billion in annualized revenue, the company told CNBC on Tuesday.

That's up from June, when Polymarket disclosed that its annual revenue run rate was north of $1 billion, following the launch of its domestic exchange in May.

Polymarket's revenue is being fueled by growth in that U.S. exchange, which operates as a separate trading venue from its decentralized finance international platform. The domestic exchange saw more than $14.5 billion in notional volume in October, up from $4 billion in June, according to data analytics platform TickerTracker.

That increase has been driven by a series of highly traded events, including the FIFA World Cup over the summer and the start of the NFL season in September. Volume has been supported by the growing popularity of Combo event contracts, which work similarly to sportsbooks' multi-leg parlays, accounted for more than 70% of volume traded on the platform on Sunday.

The revenue growth since June also comes after the company began a fresh $1 billion funding round that values Polymarket at $21 billion.

Polymarket still faces near-term headwinds, though. The Commodity Futures Trading Commission is conducting a broad investigation into the company, with questions surrounding advertising practices and whether the company in the past violated a settlement with the agency to bar U.S. residents from accessing its international exchange.

The company also may face increased scrutiny from a Democratic congress, partially due to the position of President Donald Trump's son — Donald Trump Jr. — as an advisor to the company.

But Polymarket previously told CNBC it would work with lawmakers as they begin to understand the growing prediction market industry. "We welcome scrutiny," a spokesperson said.

#138. Californians reject wealth tax for billionaires

California voters have voted to reject a wealth tax on its billionaires.

The ballot proposal would have levied a one-time tax on the state's mega wealthy to help fund healthcare. It took nearly a week of counting votes after the midterm election to determine a projection on the measure.

There are about 200 billionaires in the state, including Meta's Mark Zuckerberg, reality television star Kim Kardashian and media mogul Oprah Winfrey.

Proposition 40 targeted Californians with a wealth of more than $1.1bn (Â£800m) with a 5% tax on their total wealth, including assets such as shares as well as income. It failed amid divisions among Democrats and labour unions.

Are California voters about to impose 'world's first' tax on billionaires?

Had it passed, experts believe it would have been the world's first wealth tax targeting billionaires.

The tax would have raised about $100bn to make up for federal funding cuts to healthcare in California as a result of President Donald Trump's sweeping tax and spending bill.

Those who backed the measure had argued it would help ensure that the ultra-wealthy were contributing their fair share and helping backfill gaps in the state's budget.

The wealth tax faced fierce opposition, including from Google co-founder Sergey Brin, as well as California Governor Gavin Newsom and labour unions.

Newsom, whose tenure as governor is coming to an end, had expressed concerns that the measure could cause the wealthy to leave the state and drive out their crucial income tax revenue.

Some billionaire critics of the measure spent millions trying to crush it, with a handful even moving out of the state to evade the possible tax. Brin gave more than $100m to a political organisation funding ballot measures that could block the tax, according to the Los Angeles Times.

Dave Regan, head of the union that led the charge for the measure, said on election night that the billionaires' efforts to block the tax created "a lot of confusion and a lot of second-guessing among people who really in their hearts know what the right the thing to do is".

Regan, who is president of Service Employees International Union-United Healthcare Workers West, pledged to seek a billionaire tax once more in 2028.

The measure had odds stacked against it, with two other propositions on the ballot that would have effectively reversed it, even if it passed.

California, home to some 23 million registered voters, has the largest economy in the country and boasts the fourth largest global economy. It is home to one of the highest concentrations of billionaires in the world, but also has one of the highest wealth gaps.
