#110. Trump says FBI investigating Nvidia boss's affair with alleged Chinese spy

"He's suffering": Trump answers BBC question about Nvidia's Jensen Huang

US President Donald Trump has said the FBI is investigating the affair between the chief executive of Nvidia and an alleged Chinese spy.

Trump said he spoke to Jensen Huang on Monday following reports that the US believed he had been targeted in a Chinese espionage campaign.

"I asked him that question, 'Did you have any idea she was a spy?' He said 'I had no idea she was a spy' and I really believe him," Trump told reporters.

Beijing has rejected claims that Huang was targeted, with its foreign ministry saying the allegations were "entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China".

Speaking later at a White House press briefing, Trump told reporters, "I know the FBI is looking into" the affair but did not give further details on the investigation, or confirm when it was launched.

Trump added he thought that Huang was an "honourable guy and I feel very confident that he will be in good shape" after the investigations.

The FBI declined to comment when contacted by the BBC.

Nvidia, which has commissioned an independent investigation into the affair involving Alice Huang, alleged to be a Chinese spy, said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised.

Nvidia is the world's second most valuable company after Apple, and designs computer chips that have become central to AI technology.

The revelations knocked about $250bn (Â£190bn) off Nvidia's value on Monday as the company's share price fell by more than 4%.

US intelligence officials believe Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report. She is not related to Jensen Huang.

According to the newspaper, Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington in spring this year, warning that the Nvidia boss was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

Alice Huang was managing director of Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has had dealings with Nvidia.

Trump's remarks are the first indication of whether Nvidia's Huang knew about the woman's alleged ties to Chinese espionage.

Megaspeed's office in Singapore's central business district appeared to be operational and staffed on Tuesday.

The BBC was refused entry to its floor, but spoke to a staff member by phone who said they had no comment and directed all queries to the company's PR team.

The BBC has emailed Megaspeed several times for comment.

Nvidia, co-founded by Jensen Huang, is also a major focus in the AI technology race between the US and China, with officials in Washington treating its exports to Beijing as a matter of national security - a key reason why Huang's affair has sparked concerns.

Also on Monday, Huang and Nvidia were sued in a proposed class action lawsuit in California that accuses them of defrauding shareholders by concealing his ties with the suspected Chinese spy.

The lawsuit alleges that the company lied to the media about Jensen Huang's relationship with Alice Huang, "who is suspected of seeking to help smuggle Nvidia products to China."

The BBC has contacted Nvidia for comment on the lawsuit.

At the White House, Trump was asked several times when he was made aware of the relationship. He said he was informed "over a period of time" but was not specific.

"I hope there's no problem, I believe he is suffering greatly," the president added.

He said he spoke to Huang on the phone on Monday "just to see if he's feeling alright, how you doing and I asked him about that and he said he absolutely had no idea that the person in question was a spy".

Trump has raised concerns that allowing China to lead in AI would supercharge Beijing's military and surveillance capabilities.

The president added he was "sure" Chinese leader Xi Jinping would "ask me about it" when they next met.

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Huang has admitted that he was "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children.

He said on Monday that he was "committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America's national security" and would cooperate with the investigation the company had launched.

Huang has also been requested to appear for a joint closed-door briefing for several Congressional committees next week, however it is unclear whether Huang will appear.

A letter said the briefing would be "on Nvidia's security protocols against foreign adversary espionage and your interactions with a publicly reported People's Republic of China (PRC) intelligence asset: Megaspeed executive Alice Huang".

Nvidia has declined to comment, while the the BBC has also contacted the committees.

Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

AI stocks climb after trillions wiped from market values

Some workers the BBC spoke to at Nvidia's Santa Clara headquarters on Monday said it was business as usual, despite the intense attention on the company.

Nvidia has been around for decades, with Huang founding it with two other engineers in 1993, but its rise to one of the world's most valuable and powerful companies has only been recent.

It originally designed computer chips for gaming, but its decision to shift focus to AI development turned it into the powerhouse it is today.

Its chips have become so valuable to the AI buildout that the White House previously imposed controls on where its advanced products could be sold.

The H200, Nvidia's second most advanced semiconductor, had previously been restricted over concerns that it would give China's technology industry an edge over the US.

But Huang has long been against restricting sales to China, arguing that it would be better for the global rollout of AI to be powered by US technology.

In January, the Trump administration said it would allow the company to sell its H200 chips to Chinese customers under certain conditions.

Nvidia's most advanced AI semiconductors including the GB200 Blackwell super chip remain blocked from the Chinese market.

Additional reporting by Tessa Wong in Singapore

#108. China panel chair Moolenaar demands answers on Nvidia CEO's alleged affair with a Chinese spy

Rep. John Moolenaar, R-Mich., said on a Tuesday appearance on CNBC's "Squawk Box" that Congress needs to "get to the bottom" of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

Moolenaar, who chairs the House Permanent Select Committee on China, said he wants to know whether Huang shared privileged information with a Chinese spy; whether Huang was influenced to lobby against preventing chip sales to China; and whether China was given preference in purchasing Nvidia's limited supply of advanced chips.

And he urged a thorough internal investigation of Huang.

"If it becomes less of an investigation, more of damage control, that's not going to work because people have to have confidence," Moolenaar said.

"We're dealing with the country, the Chinese Communist Party that is a foremost adversary, that's funding wars and supporting Russia, supporting Iran, North Korea," he said. "And we have basically a Cold War race with technology. We cannot allow the premier company at the forefront of this to, in some way, be irresponsible."

Congressional scrutiny of Huang's affair has intensified since members returned to Washington on Monday.

The New York Times reported that Huang was romantically involved with Alice Huang, a Megaspeed executive who U.S. intelligence agencies have identified as a Chinese intelligence asset. Nvidia opened an investigation into its CEO on Sunday. The two Huangs are not related.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., said in a press conference on Monday that "we're going to need a full briefing specifically as it relates to the Nvidia issue so we can all get to the bottom of what has happened and what the implications are in terms of national security"

Moolenaar co-authored a letter with Florida Rep. Brian Mast and Arkansas Rep. Rick Crawford on Nov. 5 asking Huang to appear before the Select Committee on China, the House Foreign Affairs Committee and House Select Committee on Intelligence. Mast chairs the foreign affairs panel, and Crawford chairs intelligence.

"Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the PRC's access to sensitive U.S. technology and information," the three Republicans wrote.

#106. Sales of tinned food and bottled water jump after 'Be Ready' campaign launch

Sales of essential goods such as bottled water and canned food soared in the 10 days following the launch of the government's "Be Ready" emergency preparedness campaign on 22 October, research indicates.

Hand sanitiser sales were up 83%, sales of bottled water rose 67% and tinned and jarred goods climbed 37%, according to data from retail research firm Worldpanel by Numerator.

The government's public awareness campaign encouraged people to stock up in case of disruption to essential services by severe weather or cyberattacks.

However, Worldpanel said that while households were stockpiling essentials, they were not panic buying.

The government campaign encouraged people to keep three days-worth of food and water in their homes.

Wet wipes, a torch and cash were also recommended.

After the campaign launch, the equivalent of an extra 400,000 people per day bought tinned meat, Worldpanel said, although the typical amount remained at 500g, which suggests a gradual stocking-up.

"Shoppers clearly took notice of the 'Be Ready' campaign, but this wasn't panic buying, with less than a third of households buying bottled water in October," said Fraser McKevitt, head of retail and consumer insight at Worldpanel by Numerator.

"Growth came from more people adding the recommended items on the government's checklist to their weekly shop, not from shoppers piling their trolleys high, which is very much in line with what the government wanted households to do," he said.

Sales of tinned and jarred goods rose from Â£58.2m in the 10 days to 2 November 2025 to Â£79.5m over the same period in 2026.

Bottled water sales jumped from Â£13.6m to Â£22.8m compared with a year earlier and hand sanitiser sales climbed from Â£497,000 to Â£908,000.

Further evidence of stockpiling came from food firm Princes on Tuesday, which said demand for tinned tuna, meat and vegetables had risen following the government's campaign.

Princes interim chief executive Giuseppe Mastrolia said the jump in demand "further reinforces the relevance of our core categories in providing affordable, nutritious and convenient food solutions".

The "Be Ready" campaign warns that severe weather and cyber attacks could disrupt essential services, and British households need to make sure they have what they need - for example, bottled water, and long-life food that doesn't need to be kept in the fridge such as dried fruit and nuts.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham has said everyone should have "those supplies in the house to get you through those moments".

#105. Lucid misses Wall Street’s earnings expectations, expands loan from Saudi Arabia fund

Lucid Group missed Wall Street's third-quarter expectations as the embattled electric vehicle maker continues an "operational reset" aimed at cutting costs and narrowing losses.

The company on Monday also announced a $1.75 billion increase in a delayed draw term loan from Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund, the company's largest shareholder.

Lucid stock fell roughly 5% in aftermarket trading Monday.

Here's how Lucid performed in the third quarter compared with average estimates compiled by LSEG:

Despite the top- and bottom-line misses, the company's adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of a loss of $726 million beat Wall Street's expectations of a loss of $611 million for the third quarter. Its net loss for the quarter was $830.9 million, down from a loss of $978.4 million a year earlier.

Lucid's 2025 third quarter included revenue of $336.6 million, a loss of $3.31 per share and an adjusted EBITDA loss of $717.7 million.

The quarterly results are the first since Lucid CEO Silvio Napoli, who started leading the company in June, announced a restructuring plan that included implementing several cost-cutting efforts and delaying the company's upcoming midsize vehicle from the end of this year until the second half of next.

"Our transformation is beginning to show progress. We are acting with urgency, strengthening the fundamentals and driving greater discipline, while continuing to invest in the priorities to deliver profitable long-term growth," Napoli said in a release.

The company's turnaround plan includes $1.4 billion in cash flow improvements this year as well as focusing attention on a robotaxi program with Uber and Nuro, a factory that's under construction in Saudi Arabia and the midsize vehicle.

Lucid said the capital from PIF is expected to give the company "additional financial flexibility as Lucid delivers on its turnaround program." It is expected to assist in funding "operations and key investments" into the fourth quarter of 2027, including the start of production for the midsize vehicle.

The additional funds boost Lucid's total liquidity to $3.75 billion, up from $2 billion to end the third quarter.

"We are grateful for the continued financial support from our anchor shareholder, which further energizes us to remain focused on execution and on our commitments as we build a stronger, sustainable Lucid," Napoli said.

Lucid said Monday that the company expects to communicate its midterm plan and targets early next year. The EV maker in May suspended its guidance and many targets, citing new leadership and time needed to develop a turnaround plan.

#103. Anthropic heads for $1.3 trillion valuation in major stock market listing

Anthropic, the AI company behind the Claude chatbot, could be valued at $1.3 trillion (Â£1 trillion) when it lists its shares for sale on New York's Nasdaq stock exchange.

The loss-making company says it will offer its shares for between $70 and $78 each and plans to raise up to $64bn when it floats this month.

This would value Anthropic at between $1.1tn and $1.3tn which, although large, is below the $2tn some analysts had predicted weeks ago.

It is floating at a volatile time for AI firms. After last week's US midterm elections, the Nasdaq fell sharply on concerns a Democrat-led Congress could slow development of AI and the buildout of data centres.

There are also wider fears over the billions of dollars being spent on AI and whether it will pay off.

In its most recent results for the six months to the end of June, Anthropic's revenues jumped to $16.2bn from $1.1bn in the same period last year.

However, its pre-tax loss jumped to $80.7bn from $12.9bn.

Fiona Cincotta, a senior market analyst at City Index, said the $64bn Anthropic wants to raise is "fairly bullish" within "the volatility [and] concerns towards AI that we've seen in sort of particularly last week".

She told Reuters: "It's showing that they are still sort of upbeat about the outlook as far as AI is concerned and that obviously that they think that the demand is going to be there."

At $1.3tn, Anthropic's possible valuation is below SpaceX, Elon Musk's rocket, satellite and artificial intelligence company, which listed its shares in June and was valued at nearly $1.8tn.

Anthropic is just five years old. It was co-founded in 2021 by brother and sister Dario and Daniela Amodei along with five others - all of whom worked at OpenAI, the rival AI giant behind ChatGPT.

#100. 'Markets bottom on bad news' maxim rings true as chip stocks rally amid Nvidia scandal

"Markets bottom on bad news," is one of those smart-sounding rules of thumb that proves true just often enough to remain a Wall Street cliché in good standing.

Did the brutal weeks-long punishment of AI stocks culminate last week and reverse higher Monday after a pileup of adverse headlines? In the past week, American voters resoundingly opposed data-center construction across much of the country and Anthropic lowered its target IPO valuation just before a sex-and-espionage scandal struck the one-man embodiment of the entire AI economy, Nvidia's Jensen Huang.

The story of a market taking a cue from a crescendo of bad news after a nasty setback to stage a resounding bounce holds together well enough for now, after Monday's 3.4% jump in the Nasdaq-100 and 7% rip in the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index (SOX), even as Nvidia shed 4.3%.

Based purely on the tactical evidence, the tech-hardware complex came into the week pretty well washed out and primed to bounce. Numerous trading desks reported historically rare intensity of net selling in the group by institutions into last Thursday's risk purge. The Nasdaq-100 was as oversold on several metrics as it had been at the March 30 Iran-selloff low. The CBOE S&P 500 Volatility Index has put in a nice spike of the sort usually associated with a passing storm, shooting up to 28 and then receding below 20.

The recent dump in the Nasdaq and uptick in risk aversion, "had many elements of capitulation," says Laurent Laskowski, options strategist at Baird Strategas: "record options activity, a sharp spike in volatility, skew and correlations, an inverted VIX curve, and forced liquidations…The setup, therefore, is one where the market is seeing the [downside] left tail narrowing while the [upside] right tail is fattening."

Several technical-analysis gurus (including Macro Risk Advisors' John Kolovos, whose work is cited below) argue the market hasn't quite served up a "fat pitch" to buy the pullback aggressively. Maybe so. But FanGraphs calculates that in Major League Baseball only about 7% of all pitches are in the very center of the strike zone. While those fat pitches result in some 23% of all home runs hit, the other three-quarters of balls that fly over the wall come with less-perfect setups around the edges of the zone.

I wouldn't argue too strenuously against this tech-centered rally carrying on for a bit, for these reasons as well as the fact that valuations have become far less demanding. Last week, I noted Big Tech had not to that point responded to moderately stressed conditions but was getting closer, and "the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market." Monday's blip higher could be a start, with plenty still to prove.

But this AI-driven part of the market has lately shown itself less worthy of investor trust, leaving enough hints of structural damage to the bull case that faith can't be restored right away.

In a late-June column, I asked, "Was May 14 a consequential moment for this market, at least tactically?" That date, nearly six months ago, was when the S&P 500 spurted higher to reach the 7500 level for the first time; the index has never reached escape velocity since and in fact crossed above 7500 again just yesterday.

May 14 was when highly touted AI-chip upstart Cerebras came public and its shares more than doubled to a high of $386, as President Trump and China's leader were reported to have made progress on ending the Iran war. Since then, Cerebras has collapsed to $97, the semiconductor group is off 17% and the Iran conflict continues.

A month later the SpaceX IPO was another apparent culmination of New Era tech enthusiasm; it might not have been an important peak in AI excitement, but if it were the market action since would possibly resemble what we've seen since.

Perhaps a half-year reckoning period during which the market has refused to pay up for 30% annual earnings growth for two straight quarters is enough to build a new base constructed on more modest expectations. I've been focused on late-cycle tremors unsettling markets in the form of peak earnings growth, peak capex, peak index concentration, a tighter Federal Reserve and credit flareups. This all explains the market's multi-month stall, and they remain.

But it's important to remain open to the chance that these could end up amounting to mere mid-cycle turbulence, as Morgan Stanley chief investment officer Mike Wilson has been arguing.

First, though, the tape will need to show more progress than a nice, broad single-day comeback. Another rule of thumb holds that a rebound rally needs at least to carry above the 20-day moving average to prove that it's not merely a reflexive dead-cat bounce. This would require a 1.3% gain from Monday's close for the S&P 500, 3.7% for the Nasdaq-100 and 9% for the SOX.

Without pretending any clear sense of what the various Nvidia investigations will mean for its China sales, leadership or long-term earnings power, we can say for sure that the scandal arrived at a fairly opportune time for the market.

Valuations have been pounded to multi-year lows on persistent worry that current profits are unsustainable and the group remains vulnerable to any slowdown in data-center builds. This allowed, for a day, every other semiconductor stock to whistle higher as Nvidia lost some $200 billion in market cap on a blistering $80 billion in dollar-volume traded in the stock.

The S&P 500 as a whole, of course, has grown optically cheaper, too, with semis bolstering the projected earnings and investors declining to give the group credit for their sustainability.

The issue remains that less of those earnings are set to manifest as free cash flow as the hyperscalers spend manically. As this chart comparing the valuation gauges shows, the market looks a good deal less cheap on free cash flow than earnings.

A proper bull case for the market from here might be that the likes of Alphabet, Amazon, Meta and Microsoft will resume harvesting free cash flow, on which investors might place a higher multiple than they're willing to bestow on those hot but hard-to-trust chip makers. Not yet a provable argument.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "While sentiment is dire, our concern is that technology is in a 'bad oversold' condition, where seductive bounces fail to produce lasting lows. Instead, the broader equity market may need to follow technology lower, with a more widespread capitulation ultimately needed to wash out weak hands and establish a durable bottom."

-This enjoyable and illuminating review in the New Yorker of James Gleick's "The Telephone: A New History" inevitably summons parallels between the advent of telecommunications and the current AI buildout. Huge privately financed infrastructure buildout, destabilizing new habits of interaction with technology and constant calls for government regulation in exchange for near-monopoly power. The book cites an AT&T ad from 1909 calling telephones "tools of 'civilization . . . one of the most powerful influences for broadening human intelligence.'"

-Moody's Analytics economist Mark Zandi has a helpful thread suggesting AI is so far adding more jobs than it's costing the U.S. economy. The main reasons are sobering though, with most gains coming from data-center construction and wealth creation for those who own AI-dependent stocks. Cold comfort for information-economy job seekers.

-Last week's election triggering a Democratic takeover of Congress brought comparisons to the 1994 "Contract with America" Republican sweep for its magnitude and message. That election, in Bill Clinton's first term, highlighted deep societal fissures and economic instability.

With hindsight, many view the '90s as a placid era of consensus and prosperity, but between the '94 backlash election, constant corporate downsizing, scare-mongering over Federal debt levels, the 1995 Oklahoma City bombing and discomfort with technological change, that decade hit many of today's narrative beats. This Chicago Fed paper, "The decline of job security in the 1990s: Displacement, anxiety, and their effect on wage growth" can fill in some blanks for those not around for it.

Happy fifth anniversary to the peak of a pandemic speculative-tech bull market. Not quite two months before the S&P 500 hit a top in January 2022 as the Fed prepared to choke off surging inflation, perhaps by causing a recession, several key retail-trader-driven subsectors peaked.

The third week of November 2021 was the relative-performance peak in Tesla, as well as the absolute top in Coinbase and the Wisdom Tree Cloud Computing ETF (WCLD).

The five-year lookback period is one of the few time spans shy of 25 years over which the Nasdaq Composite has not outperformed the S&P 500. It's a plus that the broad market can withstand such internal speculative blowoffs, though also a good reminder that the most fun pockets of the market don't stay that way forever.

#99. Skydance outlines its $6 billion in planned cost savings. Here's the breakdown

Skydance is pulling back the curtain on how it plans to achieve at least $6 billion in cost savings in the next three years.

"By combining two global streaming services, two film and TV studio groups, two television portfolios, and overlapping corporate functions, we can reduce duplicative spend across an expense base of more than $50 billion," the company wrote in a letter to shareholders Monday as part of Paramount Skydance's third-quarter earnings release.

Skydance, the company formed by the combination of Paramount Skydance and Warner Bros. Discovery, said it projects at least $2.2 billion in cost reductions from eliminating "redundant back-office and corporate functions," including eliminating management layers and ramping the use of artificial intelligence. Those savings also account for "rationalizing" its office footprint.

Skydance expects at least $1 billion in savings from streamlining marketing and promotional spending. And around $1.4 billion in savings will come from unifying its streaming businesses and broadcast architecture and integrating back-end technology.

A final $1.4 billion in savings will be tied to "optimizing third-party spend," eliminating overlapping expenses, and consolidating purchasing decisions to one centralized group, Skydance said.

"We are confident in achieving these savings, drawing on our playbook from Paramount," the company wrote. "That program is on track to achieve over $2.7 billion in run-rate efficiencies by the end of 2026."

Some of that cost-cutting has already begun.

Last month, Skydance began offering voluntary buyouts to employees ahead of official layoffs. At the same time, the company adopted a five-day-per-week in-office policy. Additionally, last week saw the departure of eight executives — four from Warner Bros. and four from Paramount.

The company has previously said it plans to eventually merge the Paramount+ and HBO Max streaming services into a single platform.

On Monday, Skydance said it would take the first step toward that goal early next year, offering a bundle of the streaming services "at an even greater value."

Co-CEO Ynon Kreiz told investors during the company's earnings call that Skydance is also looking for ways to optimize its content budget. The company spends more than $30 billion annually on shows and films.

"As one company, we can make smarter choices about how we distribute and monetize both new content and library content," Kreiz said.

Skydance noted that it's expecting a $200 million headwind in the fourth quarter from theatrical underperformances at Warner Bros. studio related to already launched titles and upcoming releases.

The company will also utilize AI to speed up production and decrease costs to make films, co-CEO and chairman David Ellison said on the call.

"We think these tools will help unlock more original storytelling by being able to make the budget more digestible to be able to take that amount of risk," Ellison said.

The updates came alongside third-quarter results for the legacy Paramount Skydance company that included revenue of $7.03 billion, which was roughly in line with StreetAccount estimates of $7.04 billion, and adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $1 billion, topping StreetAccount estimates of $913.5 million.

However, adjusted earnings per share of 45 cents were not immediately comparable to StreetAccount EPS estimates.

The results don't include the impact of the Warner Bros. Discovery acquisition, which closed after the end of the third quarter.

Skydance detailed business units of the combined entity, including studios, direct-to-consumer and TV, and said it will provide full-year guidance during its fourth-quarter earnings report early next year.

"Going forward, we will provide guidance on an annual basis, as we prioritize long-term value creation, which we believe will be more consistent with our strategic approach," the company told shareholders. "We will continue to provide quarterly updates, including certain forward-looking commentary where applicable, which will give investors visibility into our near-term progress of the business."

#95. How GM is trying to talk used car shoppers into buying new vehicles

Affordability challenges are pushing some buyers out of the new vehicle market. Many are choosing used cars instead.

In turn, automakers are looking for ways to compete with many of the online retailers grabbing market share in the used car market and trying to bring some of those customers back into a new vehicle.

New vehicle sales tend to be more profitable for automakers, but reselling trade-ins as used vehicles is highly profitable for dealers. Used car prices are relatively strong, but newer online retailers such as Carvana and CarMax have made the market more competitive for dealers and other incumbent used car sellers.

"The internet and technology and apps on our phone have changed every aspect of retail, and probably one of the slowest areas of retail to have adopted to online and digital shopping has been the auto industry," said Ivan Feinseth, partner, chief investment officer and director of research at Tigress Financial Partners.

General Motors is a key example of automakers' attempts to address this shifting market. The automaker has revamped its certified used vehicle program, which now operates under the CarBravo name. It's essentially an online portal through which Chevrolet, Buick and GMC dealers can list their certified used vehicles and make them available to customers all over the U.S.

About 1,615 of GM's 3,700 dealers are enrolled in the program, according to the company. The only GM brand not involved is Cadillac, which maintains its own program.

"Certified" used or "preowned" vehicles are used cars that have undergone a thorough inspection and reconditioning. They typically come with guarantees and warranties from the manufacturer.

Many automakers have certified used programs for their own cars. But GM, as part of its revamp, is using CarBravo to certify vehicles made by other automakers. A lot of those cars arrive at dealerships as trade-ins, and often dealers will sell them to wholesale auctions, said John Fitzpatrick, senior manager of the CarBravo program.

"Wholesaling doesn't bring nearly the revenue that selling it to a new customer does," he said.

GM has said it is planning to net about 250,000 sales in its CarBravo program in 2026. Fitzpatrick said he doesn't expect that even half of those will end up being new car sales.

But through CarBravo, the customer gets access to GM's dealership network for service, something Fitzpatrick says is a key differentiator between its program and growing online sellers. CarBravo vehicles come with various warranties, depending on the age and mileage on the car.

GM said the program is driving more traffic into local dealerships, where purchases are typically finalized. That creates an opportunity to convert a used car shopper into a new car buyer. So far, the automaker said about 38% of the shoppers initially looking for used cars end up buying new.

A customer who wants to buy a full-size truck can look on the website and compare used Chevrolet Silverados alongside used Ford F-150s and Ram 1500s, for example. The buyer might settle on a used truck from any of those brands, but a conversation with a dealer can push them in a different direction.

"The customer picks up the phone, but in the back of their mind, they maybe always have been a traditional, more new car customer," Fitzpatrick said.

"You've shown the customer, you're going to pay $500 [per month] for this used F-150, but you may pay $510 or $515 for this new Chevy," he said. "And now all of a sudden the customer says, yeah, maybe I can do that because I'd rather have that new vehicle than the used truck."

While the average transaction price of a new vehicle is about $50,000, the average 3-year-old used car costs about $30,000 as of September, according to Cox Automotive.

However, industry analysts say customers base their purchasing decisions on the cost of the monthly payment more than on the sticker price of the car.

New cars often come with longer-term loan options than used ones, Feinseth said, which can result in lower monthly payments.

"On a 1- or 2-year-old car, you may be only stuck with a 3- or 4-year loan," he said. "On a new car, you can get a 5-year, 6-year loan."

The share of new car loans lasting 84 months hit a new record in the third quarter, at more than 1 in 4 loans, according to Edmunds.

Meanwhile, used car prices in some affordable segments have risen, according to Cox Automotive, and about three quarters of consumers express some interest in certified cars.

But the key is building the relationship with the customer, he said. About 50% of the buyers GM has gained through the program appear to be new to the brand, meaning there is no record of them in any of the automaker's customer databases. About 70% had never visited the dealership they ended up purchasing from.

"The goal is really to get that customer engaged with the store, give them the option of building a relationship with the dealer," he said. "The dealer can really help the customer figure out what works for their budget."

It's a boon for dealers too: Customers buying warranty-backed used cars often return to the dealership for service visits — which often account for about half of a dealership's profits. GM can also sell subscriptions to connected vehicle services, such as its OnStar, which provides safety, security and navigation services.

"That is a big part of General Motors' business going forward, is to sell the software and subscription services like OnStar and eventually to the cars that are enabled with Super Cruise," GM's advanced driver-assistance system, Feinseth said.

GM isn't the only automaker trying to use its certified preowned vehicle program to lure buyers.

Detroit rival Ford Motor also certifies vehicles made by other manufacturers through its Ford Blue Advantage program. It is on track to do 450,000 total sales in 2026, more than GM's 250,000 target. It didn't specify how many of those buyers end up purchasing new vehicles.

Toyota told CNBC in an email that about 40% of its used car shoppers end up buying new instead.

Honda didn't give specifics about its program. But Lance Woelfer, American Honda's vice president of automobile sales, said conversions happen "probably every day at dealerships throughout the country."

"But it also is more about that first interaction with the consumer," Woelfer said. "And there are people in any market that prefer to buy a used vehicle, but appreciate the confidence that a [certified preowned designation] can give them."

Woelfer said those customers come back to get their vehicles serviced at a rate about 18% higher than someone who buys a non-certified used car. They also return to purchase a new car at a rate about 7% to 8% higher in the future, he said.

"It's about that first relationship that allows us to then have that customer loyalty for life," he added. "And that's where our focus is. We've been doing it a long time, and we've seen a lot of benefits from the certified business."

#93. Anthropic sets IPO price range of $70 to $78, looks to start trading Nov. 19, sources say

Anthropic said it will offer 825 million shares in its upcoming IPO at a price range between $70 to $78 per share, according to an updated filing on Tuesday, bringing the five-year-old artificial intelligence lab a step closer to a potentially historic Nasdaq debut.

The company is planning to price on Nov. 18 and trade on Nov. 19, according to people familiar with the details who were granted anonymity because they were not authorized to speak publicly. Anthropic is targeting retail allocation of about 20%, the people said, a much higher percentage than the typical IPO, which is 5% to 10% for retail investors.

At the high end of the range, Anthropic would net up to $64.35 billion in proceeds through the IPO. The company could end up being worth up to $1.405 trillion on a fully diluted basis, up from the $965 billion valuation it announced as part of a funding round in May.

Anthropic said it has entered into an agreement with Advanced Micro Devices, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company to purchase $26 billion of the company's Class N common stock in a private placement at a price per share equal to the IPO price. The shares sold in the private placement will not be registered in the offering and will not be subject to lock-up agreements with the underwriters, the company said.

As Anthropic executives get set to pitch the offering to investors, they have to contend with a tumultuous political environment, following last week's midterm elections that included a Democratic sweep in Congress and the passage of numerous statewide ballot measures that could slow the AI buildout. They're also facing a public market that's grown increasingly skeptical of the AI trade on concern that rising interest rates will make debt unaffordable for companies that need it for costly infrastructure purchases.

CNBC reported last week that some Anthropic investors were bracing for a lower valuation than had previously been expected. While Anthropic had been looking toward a $2 trillion valuation until recently, shareholders told CNBC that even $1.5 trillion is now in doubt, with one investor calling that number a "stretch."

Should Anthropic reach the Nasdaq this month as planned, under ticker symbol "ANT," the debut would come roughly five months after SpaceX went public in a record offering that raised $85.7 billion. Elon Musk's reusable rocket and AI company has a market cap of just under $2 trillion, around its valuation at the time of the IPO.

Institutional demand for Anthropic is even stronger than it was for SpaceX at this point in the process, the people said. The company's book is already covered at the $70 to $78 per share range on an allocable basis, they said.

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the AI lab in 2021 after he and a small group of researchers defected from OpenAI, now its chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, and the company primarily sells its products and services to other businesses.

On Monday, Anthropic introduced two new AI models, Claude Fable 5.2 and Claude Mythos 5.2, the first time the company has released so-called "frontier" models since Amodei called to slow down the pace of AI development in September. Anthropic said the models went through rigorous safety and alignment testing, and that the company developed new evaluations and post-launch monitoring tools ahead of the release.

For the nine months ended Sept. 30, Anthropic said it generated $33.24 billion in revenue up roughly 1,200% from the same period last year. It reported a $104 billion net loss during the nine-month period, though most of that was due to the change in fair value of convertible notes and derivative liabilities. Anthropic lost $25.1 billion over the same stretch in 2025, the filing said.

Anthropic said in its amended filing on Tuesday that its run-rate revenue was roughly $81 billion at the end of October, up $10 billion from the previous month.

"Our aim is to help humankind chart a safe course through this technological evolution, open new frontiers of discovery, and bring forth an age of miracles," Anthropic's co-founders wrote in the prospectus. "Everyone who owns our stock, whether an institution or an individual, becomes part of that effort. We hope you will share our ambition to see it though."

#86. Pete Hegseth decries impeachment push, defends Iran war handling

Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth on Tuesday railed against the Republican congressman who introduced articles of impeachment against him and aggressively defended his leadership in the Iran war and other military operations.

"What one disgruntled and defeated congressman is attempting is not oversight, and it's not accountability. It's a political spectacle, a personal manifesto driven by ego and resentment," Hegseth said a day after Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., put forward the eight impeachment articles.

"It's a farce, and it's a stunt, and everybody knows it," Hegseth said during his first Pentagon news conference in six months.

The articles accuse Hegseth of waging war in contravention of Congress, carrying out extrajudicial killings tied to boat strikes in the Caribbean against purported drug smugglers, and suppressing free speech by targeting Sen. Mark Kelly, D-Ariz., a veteran.

Massie, a libertarian who lost his Republican primary after clashing with President Donald Trump, kicked off the impeachment battle less than a week after a blue-wave midterm that will flip House and Senate control to Democrats.

Rep. Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., who is poised to be speaker of the House in the next Congress, on Sunday said Democrats are a "hard yes" on impeaching Hegseth.

The House is set to vote Tuesday on a procedural motion related to the impeachment resolution. If it passes, the measure will proceed to a debate and vote. Impeachment resolutions are privileged, meaning the House must act on them, table them or vote to refer them to committee within two legislative days.

Hegseth accused Massie of performing a "stunt" that sends dangerous signals to America's enemies.

"It puts a target on the backs of American warriors by signaling that Congress will undermine their missions in real time," Hegseth said. "This is not patriotism. This is sabotage."

Trump piled on, writing in a Truth Social post right after Hegseth's presser ended that the Pentagon chief "gets an 'A' PLUS, PLUS, PLUS for his defense against a disgruntled, angry, defeated, former Congressman, Thomas Massie, a TOTAL LOSER!"

Trump, who was impeached twice during his first presidential term, said Monday that he would stand by Hegseth even if the House impeaches him.

Republicans still hold a slim 218-214 majority in the House, but it is unclear if the rest of the GOP conference will be able to band together to crush Massie's impeachment resolution. Some Republicans, notably including Vice President JD Vance, have directly blamed their midterm losses on the Iran war, which continues to see intermittent fighting amid an uneasy stalemate.

If Hegseth is impeached, the Senate will hold a trial and vote on whether to remove him from office. A two-thirds majority is required for conviction and removal. No U.S. Cabinet secretary has ever been removed by the Senate.

— CNBC's Garrett Downs contributed reporting.

#80. Jensen Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy further complicates Nvidia’s China problem

Following Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's admission on Sunday to an extramarital affair with alleged Chinese spy Alice Huang, the chipmaker's relationship to China is about to come under intense scrutiny in Washington, D.C.

Since October 2022, when the Biden administration announced sweeping controls to limit China's ability to obtain advanced U.S. chips, Nvidia has seen its business diminish in the world's second-largest economy. Months after an even stricter set of rules was implemented by the Trump administration, Huang said in October 2025, "We are 100% out of China."

The recent allegations against Huang, while throwing the chipmaker into crisis, have also brought attention to the Nvidia CEO's potential influence over U.S. policy on chip sales to China, where its business once flourished. Of particular interest is whether restricted Nvidia chips have reached Chinese companies through customers like Singapore-based Megaspeed, the company linked to Alice Huang.

The New York Times first reported that the Chinese businesswoman was a spy for China's Ministry of State Security, according to people briefed on the intelligence circulated in a memo among the Trump administration in the spring. China has called the allegation a smear.

Jensen Huang has been closely aligned with President Donald Trump during his second administration, helping advise the White House on artificial intelligence safety regulations, while also pushing for looser restrictions on selling AI chips to China. In December, Trump said Nvidia would be allowed to ship its H200 chips to China, provided the U.S. got a 25% cut of sales. In March, Huang told reporters that Nvidia had "received purchase orders, and we're in the process of restarting our manufacturing."

Huang was a last-minute addition to the roster of tech executives who joined Trump on a trip to China in May, after the president was briefed on intelligence concerns, according to the Times.

"In 35 years, I haven't seen a chip CEO with this much direct influence on export policy," said Patrick Moorhead, CEO at Moor Insights & Strategy, in an email.

Since last week's midterm elections that included sweeping victories across the country for Democrats, the mood in Washington has changed. Three House committees have requested closed-door briefings with the Nvidia CEO about his relationship with Megaspeed.

Democratic lawmakers, who are about to take power in the House and Senate, have said publicly that Congress will investigate and seek answers. Trump said Monday that he was "amazed" by the allegations and that Huang told him he "had no idea that she was a spy." The FBI is now looking into the issue, Trump said at a White House briefing.

It's been a long four years for Nvidia in China.

Before the initial export controls in 2022, China made up nearly a quarter of Nvidia's revenue. Nvidia responded to the Biden-era restrictions by redesigning some of its products to comply with the new regulations.

"It was a turbulent month or so as the company went upside down to re-engineer" the products, Huang told CNBC in a 2023 interview. Still, he was confident the company could continue selling chips to China. "I think we're going to be just fine in the ability to serve the customers there," he said.

After the Biden administration tightened export restrictions again, Nvidia redesigned another AI graphics processing unit for China, the H20. Following reports that Chinese model developer DeepSeek was using restricted Nvidia chips, the Trump administration limited sales of H20. Nvidia revealed it would incur $5.5 billion in costs related to canceled orders for the redesigned chip.

However, despite saying in its forecasts that "we're assuming 0% for China," the company maintained business in the region. In the last fiscal year, Nvidia reported that about 9% of its revenue came from China, including Hong Kong, although that includes revenue from segments outside of restricted data center chips, such as gaming, auto and industrial. Nvidia's China revenue was 8% in the most recent quarter.

A report in May from short seller Culper Research alleged Nvidia's China exposure was much higher. The firm said that more than 20% of Nvidia's fiscal 2026 compute revenue was driven by China, supported by "illegal GPU diversion and Southeast Asian intermediaries," according to the report.

A loophole allows Chinese companies to rent Nvidia GPUs in data centers, even when they're restricted from buying them outright. For example, a Chinese company could lease Nvidia computing power in Europe by renting server capacity from a non-Chinese neocloud or data center operator that's exempt from export controls.

Nvidia didn't respond to a request for comment for this story.

Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer based in Singapore, has been accused of diverting its own supply of Nvidia's restricted AI chips to Chinese companies. Megaspeed denies wrongdoing and said inspections found no evidence of violations.

In 2025, Nvidia changed the way it reported regional sales to categorize by customer headquarters, instead of by where the products were billed. Nvidia also stopped reporting Singapore sales during the same period.

"Whether Blackwell chips reached China is a Megaspeed question that predates this week," said Moorhead, pointing to Bloomberg reporting in December 2025 that found Megaspeed had imported 50,000 more Nvidia chips than Nvidia tallied in its data centers.

In 2025, a House Select Committee opened an investigation into whether China's DeepSeek operates on Nvidia's chips.

A separate investigation by the U.S. Commerce Department was centered on Megaspeed. That probe was abruptly transferred to the Justice Department, the Times reported, following an email from Nvidia's general counsel to the Commerce Department seeking information about the investigation.

The restrictions aren't just coming from the U.S. China has continued to limit the amount of Nvidia chips it will allow into the country, keeping Nvidia's China sales numbers down. The U.S. has licensed H200 sales to Alibaba, Tencent, ByteDance and other Chinese companies, but Beijing hasn't approved large-scale imports, Moorhead told CNBC. Nvidia said H200 shipments to China made up less than 1% of data center revenue for the quarter that ended in July.

Moorhead expects more export control legislation to pass as a result of the latest turmoil. Proposals could allow Congress oversight of chip export licenses or require chipmakers to install security mechanisms to track the location of chips to determine if they've found their way to China.

"Every China license now gets read through this story," Moorhead said. "Jensen has been Nvidia's lead voice on China, and that voice is weaker."

— CNBC's Kif Leswing and Seema Mody contributed reporting to this report.

Correction: Three House committees have requested closed-door briefings with the Nvidia CEO about his relationship with Megaspeed. An earlier version misstated the time element.
